=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a sequence A of non-negative integers of length N, and an integer K. It is guaranteed that the binomial coefficient \dbinom{N}{K} is at most 10^6.
When choosing K distinct elements from A, find the maximum possible value of the XOR of the K chosen elements.
That is, find \underset{1\leq i_1\lt i_2\lt \ldots\lt i_K\leq N}{\max} A_{i_1}\oplus A_{i_2}\oplus \ldots \oplus A_{i_K}.

About XOR

For non-negative integers A,B, the XOR A \oplus B is defined as follows:


- In the binary representation of A \oplus B, the bit corresponding to 2^k (k \ge 0) is 1 if and only if exactly one of the bits corresponding to 2^k in A and B is 1, and is 0 otherwise.


For example, 3 \oplus 5 = 6 (in binary notation: 011 \oplus 101 = 110).
In general, the XOR of K integers p_1, \dots, p_k is defined as (\cdots((p_1 \oplus p_2) \oplus p_3) \oplus \cdots \oplus p_k). It can be proved that it does not depend on the order of p_1, \dots, p_k.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1\leq K\leq N\leq 2\times 10^5
- 0\leq A_i<2^{60}
- \dbinom{N}{K}\leq 10^6
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 2
3 2 6 4

Sample Output 1

7

Here are six ways to choose two distinct elements from (3,2,6,4).

- (3,2): The XOR is 3\oplus 2 = 1.
- (3,6): The XOR is 3\oplus 6 = 5.
- (3,4): The XOR is 3\oplus 4 = 7.
- (2,6): The XOR is 2\oplus 6 = 4.
- (2,4): The XOR is 2\oplus 4 = 6.
- (6,4): The XOR is 6\oplus 4 = 2.

Hence, the maximum possible value is 7.

Sample Input 2

10 4
1516 1184 1361 2014 1013 1361 1624 1127 1117 1759

Sample Output 2

2024

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Option 1: Use itertools.combinations. Code:

import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N,K = map(int,input().split())
    A = list(map(int,input().split()))
    total = 0
    for a in A:
        total ^= a
    L = min(K, N-K)
    if L == 0:
        print(total)
        return
    if K <= N-K:
        # enumerate selected
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(range(N), L):
            x = 0
            for i in comb:
                x ^= A[i]
            if x > ans:
                ans = x
    else:
        # enumerate complement
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(range(N), L):
            x = 0
            for i in comb:
                x ^= A[i]
            val = total ^ x
            if val > ans:
                ans = val
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__": main()

Let's decide final code:

```python
import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, K = data[0], data[1]
    A = data[2:2+N]

    total = 0
    for a in A:
        total ^= a

    L = min(K, N - K)
    if L == 0:
        print(total)
        return

    if K <= N - K:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(range(N), L):
            x = 0
            for i in comb:
                x ^= A[i]
            if x > ans:
                ans = x
    else:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(range(N), L):
            x = 0
            for i in comb:
                x ^= A[i]
            v = total ^ x
            if v > ans:
                ans = v

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Now, code becomes:

from itertools import combinations

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, K = data[0], data[1]
    A = data[2:2+N]

    total = 0
    for a in A:
        total ^= a

    L = min(K, N - K)
    if L == 0:
        print(total)
        return

    if K <= N - K:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(A, L):
            x = 0
            for v in comb:
                x ^= v
            if x > ans:
                ans = x
    else:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(A, L):
            x = 0
            for v in comb:
                x ^= v
            val = total ^ x
            if val > ans:
                ans = val
    print(ans)

This is simpler and faster. Is there any subtlety with combinations of values and duplicate values causing fewer combinations? Let's confirm itertools.combinations does not filter duplicates; it's based on positions. From docs: "The combination tuples are emitted in lexicographic ordering according to the order of the input iterable. So, if the input iterable is sorted, the combination tuples will be produced in sorted order. Elements are treated as unique based on their position, not on their value. So if the input elements are unique, there will be no repeat values in each combination." Yes. So good.

Now, if A is a list, `combinations(A, L)` will generate tuples of length L. For L=0, combinations(A,0) yields one empty tuple; but we handle. If L=0 and we didn't handle, direct? K=N? That's okay because XOR same; but we still iterate duplicates. Could we deduplicate values to reduce? No, because positions with same value are distinct but XOR same; for maximizing XOR, duplicates don't matter? Wait, if there are duplicate values, choosing one duplicate vs another yields same XOR for selected? Since values identical, yes, for any subset, replacing one duplicate with another identical value yields same XOR. Therefore duplicates as values are interchangeable. But the number of available duplicates matters for feasibility of choosing K distinct elements? If we deduplicate, we might lose multiplicity and think a value is available when it's not? Maybe we can write a helper function to compute max XOR for combinations to avoid duplicating code:

def max_xor_combinations(iterable, L):
    ans = 0
    for comb in combinations(iterable, L):
        x = 0
        for v in comb:
            x ^= v
        if x > ans:
            ans = x
    return ans

Then:
if K <= N-K:
    ans = max_xor_combinations(A, L)
else:
    ans = 0
    for comb in combinations(A, L):
        x = 0
        for v in comb:
            x ^= v
        val = total ^ x
        if val > ans: ans = val

This avoids 200k tuple creations. I'll include it? Let's see final code:

import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, K = data[0], data[1]
    A = data[2:2+N]

    total = 0
    for a in A:
        total ^= a

    L = min(K, N - K)
    if L == 0:
        print(total)
        return

    if L == 1:
        if K <= N - K:
            print(max(A))
        else:
            ans = 0
            for a in A:
                v = total ^ a
                if v > ans:
                    ans = v
            print(ans)
        return

    if K <= N - K:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(A, L):
            x = 0
            for v in comb:
                x ^= v
            if x > ans:
                ans = x
    else:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(A, L):
            x = 0
            for v in comb:
                x ^= v
            val = total ^ x
            if val > ans:
                ans = val

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

This is robust. But is L==1 special case necessary? It might be considered extra but fine. However, if K=1,N=1, L=0 handled. If K=1,N=2, L=1, K<=N-K (1<=1) true, print(max(A)). Correct. If K=N-1,N=2, K=1,N=2, same. If K=N-1,N=3, K=2>N-K=1, complement loop. Correct.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
from itertools import combinations

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, K = data[0], data[1]
    A = data[2:2 + N]

    total = 0
    for a in A:
        total ^= a

    L = min(K, N - K)

    if L == 0:
        print(total)
        return

    if L == 1:
        if K <= N - K:
            print(max(A))
        else:
            ans = 0
            for a in A:
                v = total ^ a
                if v > ans:
                    ans = v
            print(ans)
        return

    if K <= N - K:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(A, L):
            x = 0
            for v in comb:
                x ^= v
            if x > ans:
                ans = x
    else:
        ans = 0
        for comb in combinations(A, L):
            x = 0
            for v in comb:
                x ^= v
            val = total ^ x
            if val > ans:
                ans = val

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
